OUR HAPPY CHAOS — 100 COMPLETE VIDEO PROMPTS
Version: 3 November 2026, CARTOON V2 reference and emotion-reviewed edition. Latest audio choice: ALL episodes include generated background music, character voices/reactions and scene sounds. No 50/50 silent split remains.

HOW TO USE
Copy one complete numbered block. Attach/import its raw image URL through your generator's reference control; merely pasting a URL into text may not load an image. Select vertical 9:16 and 30 seconds in the interface as well as the prompt. Use the labeled family image as an identity reference, not the opening frame. The FIRST FRAME paragraph specifies the actual opening shot. Generate that opening in the SAME CARTOON V2 style before animating; never start from a realistic human image. Select a 30-second generation duration in the provider UI: the previously reviewed 20-second result did not meet the requested length. Disable any photoreal/live-action preset if one is selected; use a cartoon/animation style option if available. No paid jobs have been submitted and these are untested generation instructions. If your provider cannot render 30 seconds in one job, retain the exact timeline in linked clips and edit them to 30 seconds; do not compress the story into a shorter setting. Review each result for face drift, unwanted extra cast, garbled labels, voice changes, missing beats and pacing. Names alone do not lock faces. Voice descriptions encourage similarity; an approved reusable voice/audio reference is needed for stronger voice continuity. Independently generated music may vary even with a repeated music description. Replacing the entire audio track later removes speech and effects along with the generated music.

SERIES CANON
Same original family in every episode. U.S. suburban home is their base; European scenes are vacations, not a change of identity. No aging between episodes. Wife Emma 31; husband Jack 35; daughter Lily 7; son Noah 5; dog Buddy. Warm affectionate comedy, shared effort and small domestic reversals. Outfit changes are intentional episode wardrobe only. Use original series branding, original music and the approved family characters.



EMOTIONAL CHARACTER DIRECTION
Emma is expressive and, in selected misunderstanding stories, sensitive to feeling overlooked. This is her individual fictional personality, not a statement about women. Jack stays calm when she is upset: he notices, listens, offers reassurance and apologizes when appropriate. A hug follows an open-arm invitation or Emma leaning toward him; it is not a substitute for resolving the misunderstanding. Keep tears subtle and brief, never constant. Some scenes use happy tears instead. Both partners retain humor, agency and dignity. Each episode below has its own six-stage emotional progression and physical acting instructions.

PROMPT 001 — Mascara Face Revenge
==============================================================================
FORMAT: Exactly 30 seconds. Vertical 9:16 portrait, target 1080x1920. Fill the portrait frame; no landscape image inside borders, no letterboxing. Fast-paced natural movement and tight comic timing.

CHARACTER REFERENCE IMAGE URL (attach this actual image):
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/relnoorain2-droid/Big-Earl-Eats-America/refs/heads/main/Our-Happy-Chaos/our-happy-chaos-cartoon-reference-v2.png

IDENTITY LOCK: Use ONLY the attached CARTOON V2 named family image for BOTH identity and cartoon rendering style. Do not combine it with earlier realistic family images or live-action opening frames.
Emma, adult wife, age 31: the reference blonde woman with shoulder-length side-parted golden-blonde hair, bright blue eyes, softly heart-shaped face, rosy cheeks and warm playful smile; adult proportions, shorter and slimmer than Jack.
Jack, adult husband, age 35: the APPROVED broad, sturdy man with warm brown eyes, dark chestnut side-parted hair, broad rounded-square face, dense FULL rounded dark beard extending below his chin and connected mustache. Never substitute the earlier slim, stubbled husband; never shorten or remove his beard.
CAST IN THIS EPISODE: Emma, Jack only. Do not show Lily, Noah, Buddy. Preserve faces, skin tones, eye colors, hair, child ages, relative heights and Buddy's markings across every shot. Maintain Jack's full beard. Never turn children into miniature adults. Do not reproduce the reference title, labels, borders or group-photo pose. Background passersby, if any, are indistinct and never become featured cast.

WARDROBE: Emma: loose light-beige T-shirt with one small cute yellow chick graphic, light-blue jeans; approved Emma face and blonde hair; Jack: grayish-blue T-shirt with a cartoon shark wearing sunglasses and yellow swim trunks, blue jeans; approved Jack face and FULL beard. Keep outfits unchanged within this episode except an explicitly shown clothing action.

STYLE AND LOCATION: UNMISTAKABLE STYLIZED 3D CARTOON throughout, matching the attached CARTOON V2 reference from the FIRST FRAME to the LAST FRAME. Every person, animal, prop and location is a designed animated asset, never a filmed actor or photographic background. Use expressive enlarged cartoon eyes, rounded cheek planes, simplified small noses, appealing slightly enlarged heads with clearly adult or child proportions, smooth matte skin, sculpted hair locks, Jack's dense full rounded beard as a sculpted shape with a few clean grooves, and simplified fabric folds. Keep recognizability and the V2 character proportions stable across shots. No visible pores, realistic wrinkles, photoreal facial hair, individual micro-hair strands, fabric microtexture, live-action footage, realistic human casting, uncanny human faces, or blending/morphing between realism and cartoon. Use bright soft animated lighting, clear colors and gently exaggerated readable gestures. Physical actions remain brisk and natural; style stays cartoon. Recurring family bathroom in the same U.S. suburban house: beige/light-brown square wall tiles with white grout, white ceiling with recessed spotlights, white vanity, chrome faucet, soft even vanity lighting. Keep fixtures and tile scale consistent.

FIRST FRAME IMAGE PROMPT: Create one still opening composition in vertical 9:16 using the identities and wardrobe above. Capture the first instant of the following setup, not a collage of its successive actions. Emma is close to the camera-as-mirror, eyes looking slightly upward, black mascara wand at her right eyelashes, mouth stretched into the exaggerated O from the original concept. Jack is initially outside the frame. Show beige square tiles, white grout, white ceiling spotlights and even soft vanity light. No collage, no name labels, no title card.

CAMERA: STATIC LOCKED-OFF mirror-perspective close-up throughout: camera IS the mirror, not a camera filming a mirror. Emma fills foreground center-left; Jack can enter and leave in the right background. No pan, tilt, zoom, cuts, handheld movement or duplicate reflected bodies. Keep the vanity props within reach. Emma uses her own right hand at her anatomical right eye; do not swap hands or eyes between actions.

ACTION TIMELINE — fill every interval with purposeful action; a longer block contains linked actions and quick reactions, not one held expression.
[00-03s] Emma applies mascara to her right eyelashes, looking upward with her mouth in a huge O.
  Emotional beat: focused exaggerated mascara face.
[03-08s] Jack enters from screen right, notices her face and copies it while touching his eyebrow.
  Emotional beat: Jack mischievous amusement.
[08-13s] Emma catches him in the mirror, closes her mouth and lowers the black wand with a deadpan stare.
  Emotional beat: Emma sharply annoyed deadpan.
[13-19s] She takes a dark-blue disposable razor, puffs her cheeks and makes exaggerated gentle shaving strokes over cheek and chin.
  Emotional beat: Emma determined comic retaliation.
[19-25s] Jack recoils, checks his own full beard and backs out to screen right; Emma keeps her serious shaving face until he disappears.
  Emotional beat: Jack startled and uneasy.
[25-30s] Emma puts the razor down and resumes mascara with the identical opening O-face; finish on the matching opening pose.
  Emotional beat: Emma privately triumphant, then focused again.

EMOTION / PERFORMANCE: Animate the emotional beat through eyes, eyebrows, mouth and shoulders while the action continues. Show anticipation before the reveal and a clear change afterward; do not paste a constant smile onto every face. Mild irritation uses a lowered brow, pressed lips or folded arms; surprise uses a quick brow lift and intake of breath; relief releases the shoulders and softens the mouth; happiness warms the eyes before the smile. Keep micro-reactions about 0.4-1 second inside the existing timing, without adding pauses or extra runtime. Do not add crying where this episode does not call for it. A comic pout, a surprised blink or a fond smile is enough. Jack may be surprised, confused or sheepish, but never responds to a hurt partner or child with angry shouting. Keep him patient, approachable and capable of laughing at himself. VOICE ACTING: Match the written emotional beat: warm and low for reassurance, brighter for delight, a soft breath for surprise, restrained rather than shrill when hurt. Keep the existing speaker identity and exact dialogue. Music dips under vulnerable moments and returns warmly as feelings resolve; effects must not mock a sincere tear.

AUDIO: Generate ORIGINAL instrumental background music: playful pizzicato strings, light acoustic plucks, soft bass and crisp restrained percussion, brisk approximately 112 BPM, friendly family-comedy theme, no recognizable existing melody, no sung lyrics. Begin quietly under the opening action, use a brief musical dip around the reveal at 13-19s, then a small resolving phrase under the ending. Music stays clearly below voices; no loud wall-to-wall track. Scene sounds: quiet room tone, wand or brush handling, faucet drips only when visible, cloth swishes, synchronized only to visible causes. Never add offscreen events or canned laughter. Keep reactions brief and natural. Preserve the original no-dialogue concept: Jack gives a brief amused snicker at 05s and a surprised breath at 20s; Emma gives one restrained exhale at 10s. Add gentle wand handling, subtle footsteps, a brief quiet beat at her deadpan stare, light stylized razor scrape during the shaving pantomime, and retreating footsteps. No spoken words or narrator.

PACING / CONTINUITY CHECKS: Hook already happening in the first second. No slow motion, slow entrances, lengthy walking, frozen smiles or idle filler. Keep every action inside the locked mirror shot; use brisk movement and facial changes rather than cuts or unnatural speed-ups. Let key reactions read briefly, then advance the action. Track the same props in the same hands; show every transfer. Hands, fingers and tools stay anatomically coherent; no teleporting objects, body morphing or extra limbs. Keep gestures affectionate rather than cruel. No subtitles, speech bubbles, watermarks, brand logos or generated UI text. Finish the specified payoff by 30s without an extra end card.
SPECIFIC ORIGINAL-PROMPT LOCK: The razor action is playful gentle pantomime on Emma's already smooth cheeks and chin, with no injury or new facial hair. It never touches Jack; his full beard remains unchanged. Keep the camera locked despite the general fast-pacing requirement. The last two seconds match Emma's opening mascara pose for a loop.
